========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… what’s bringing me in is that lately I feel like I’m constantly running on a motor. Like I don’t really stop. I’ll finish one thing at school, or at home, and instead of pausing or feeling done, I immediately jump into the next thing. It’s like there’s no off switch.

And I know everybody’s busy, especially working in education, but this feels different. I’m always moving, always onto the next task, the next thought, the next thing that needs doing. Even when there isn’t something urgent, I seem to create something to do. I just can’t seem to shut myself down, and it’s gotten exhausting.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it’s definitely not new. I mean, I can’t say I had the language for it when I was a kid, but looking back, it was always there.

My mother used to joke—well, half-joke, half-complain—that even in elementary school I was basically “teaching the class from my seat,” because I was always popping up out of my chair, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, or jumping in before the teacher had even finished giving directions. I wasn’t trying to be rude, exactly. It was more like I heard the first part and my brain and body were already moving before the rest of me caught up.

So this isn’t something that just started recently, or just because of changes in the family or stress now. It’s been part of how I’ve been wired for as long as I can remember.

Yes. Yeah, unfortunately it’s not just one setting. It’s showing up in both places, and that’s part of why I’m here, because I think I kept telling myself, “Well, work is just stressful,” or “home is just complicated,” but it’s bigger than that.

At work, it’s become serious. The complaints have gotten to the point where I was put on a corrective plan, which is… honestly embarrassing to say out loud. I’m a special education teacher, and I care a lot about what I do, and I’m trying to be helpful and responsive, but the way I jump in or push ahead or keep moving seems to rub people the wrong way or make them feel like I’m not listening. So now it’s not just coworkers being annoyed — it’s formal.

And then at home, my spouse has told me pretty clearly that the caregiving routines are harder because everyone’s not just managing the actual responsibilities, they’re also managing my energy. Like I come in trying to help, but I can make the whole thing feel more chaotic. I don’t mean to, but that’s the impact.

So yes, it’s causing real problems. I feel like I’m damaging trust with people at work and at home, even when my intention is to help. That’s the part that’s been really hard to sit with.

I mean, I can’t say I had the language for it when I was a kid, but looking back, it was always there.\n\nMy mother used to joke—well, half-joke, half-complain—that even in elementary school I was basically “teaching the class from my seat,” because I was always popping up out of my chair, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, or jumping in before the teacher had even finished giving directions. I wasn’t trying to be rude, exactly. It was more like I heard the first part and my brain and body were already moving before the rest of me caught up.\n\nSo this isn’t something that just started recently, or just because of changes in the family or stress now. It’s been part of how I’ve been wired for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Right now, would you say these feelings of restlessness and impulsivity are causing real problems for you both at work and at home, not just one or the other?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, unfortunately it’s not just one setting. It’s showing up in both places, and that’s part of why I’m here, because I think I kept telling myself, “Well, work is just stressful,” or “home is just complicated,” but it’s bigger than that.\n\nAt work, it’s become serious. The complaints have gotten to the point where I was put on a corrective plan, which is… honestly embarrassing to say out loud. I’m a special education teacher, and I care a lot about what I do, and I’m trying to be helpful and responsive, but the way I jump in or push ahead or keep moving seems to rub people the wrong way or make them feel like I’m not listening. So now it’s not just coworkers being annoyed — it’s formal.\n\nAnd then at home, my spouse has told me pretty clearly that the caregiving routines are harder because everyone’s not just managing the actual responsibilities, they’re also managing my energy. Like I come in trying to help, but I can make the whole thing feel more chaotic. I don’t mean to, but that’s the impact.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing real problems. I feel like I’m damaging trust with people at work and at home, even when my intention is to help. That’s the part that’s been really hard to sit with.\n\n[Last Question]\nDoes this high-energy, can't-stop feeling ever come in distinct episodes—lasting days at a time—where you also notice your mood is unusually elevated or irritable and you need much less sleep than normal, or is it more of a constant, unchanging pattern for you all the time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels much more constant than like episodes. I mean, I don’t really think of it as, “Oh, for three days I was suddenly different,” and then I came back down. It’s more like… this is just the speed I’m stuck at most of the time.

What brought me in is exactly that feeling of running on a motor, like I’ll finish something at school or at home and instead of my brain saying, “Okay, pause,” I’m already moving into the next thing. It doesn’t feel like a burst that comes and goes as much as a pattern I’m living in. Like there’s always another task, another thing to fix, another thing to jump into, and I have a really hard time shutting myself off.

I don’t think so, no. At least not in the way you’re describing.

I mean, I’ve definitely annoyed people, and I’ve been corrected plenty of times for interrupting or moving too fast or not waiting my turn, especially in structured settings. But I wouldn’t describe myself as someone who was deliberately defying authority or trying to break rules just to break them. It’s more like I would act before thinking, and then realize afterward that I’d stepped on a rule or pushed past someone’s boundary.

I haven’t had, you know, a pattern of aggression or serious trouble with the law or anything like that. And at work, the problems have been more about how I come across and how hard it is for me to slow down, not me refusing to follow rules on purpose. So… no, I wouldn’t say there’s been an ongoing pattern like that beyond the impulsive stuff.

It’s showing up in both places, and that’s part of why I’m here, because I think I kept telling myself, “Well, work is just stressful,” or “home is just complicated,” but it’s bigger than that.\n\nAt work, it’s become serious. The complaints have gotten to the point where I was put on a corrective plan, which is… honestly embarrassing to say out loud. I’m a special education teacher, and I care a lot about what I do, and I’m trying to be helpful and responsive, but the way I jump in or push ahead or keep moving seems to rub people the wrong way or make them feel like I’m not listening. So now it’s not just coworkers being annoyed — it’s formal.\n\nAnd then at home, my spouse has told me pretty clearly that the caregiving routines are harder because everyone’s not just managing the actual responsibilities, they’re also managing my energy. Like I come in trying to help, but I can make the whole thing feel more chaotic. I don’t mean to, but that’s the impact.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing real problems. I feel like I’m damaging trust with people at work and at home, even when my intention is to help. That’s the part that’s been really hard to sit with.\n\nDoctor: Does this high-energy, can't-stop feeling ever come in distinct episodes—lasting days at a time—where you also notice your mood is unusually elevated or irritable and you need much less sleep than normal, or is it more of a constant, unchanging pattern for you all the time?\n\nPatient: It feels much more constant than like episodes. I mean, I don’t really think of it as, “Oh, for three days I was suddenly different,” and then I came back down. It’s more like… this is just the speed I’m stuck at most of the time.\n\nWhat brought me in is exactly that feeling of running on a motor, like I’ll finish something at school or at home and instead of my brain saying, “Okay, pause,” I’m already moving into the next thing. It doesn’t feel like a burst that comes and goes as much as a pattern I’m living in. Like there’s always another task, another thing to fix, another thing to jump into, and I have a really hard time shutting myself off.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness and impulsivity, have you ever had ongoing patterns of defying authority, breaking rules, being aggressive, or getting into serious trouble—like at school, work, or with the law—that go beyond just being impulsive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. At least not in the way you’re describing.\n\nI mean, I’ve definitely annoyed people, and I’ve been corrected plenty of times for interrupting or moving too fast or not waiting my turn, especially in structured settings. But I wouldn’t describe myself as someone who was deliberately defying authority or trying to break rules just to break them. It’s more like I would act before thinking, and then realize afterward that I’d stepped on a rule or pushed past someone’s boundary.\n\nI haven’t had, you know, a pattern of aggression or serious trouble with the law or anything like that. And at work, the problems have been more about how I come across and how hard it is for me to slow down, not me refusing to follow rules on purpose. I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, I can lose the thread of what I’m doing sometimes, but when I think about what’s actually creating the problems, it feels much more tied to the restlessness and the jumping in too quickly than to, like, sitting there unable to focus.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Does this high-energy, can't-stop feeling ever come in distinct episodes—lasting days at a time—where you also notice your mood is unusually elevated or irritable and you need much less sleep than normal, or is it more of a constant, unchanging pattern for you all the time?\n\nPatient: It feels much more constant than like episodes. I mean, I don’t really think of it as, “Oh, for three days I was suddenly different,” and then I came back down. It’s more like… this is just the speed I’m stuck at most of the time.\n\nWhat brought me in is exactly that feeling of running on a motor, like I’ll finish something at school or at home and instead of my brain saying, “Okay, pause,” I’m already moving into the next thing. It doesn’t feel like a burst that comes and goes as much as a pattern I’m living in. Like there’s always another task, another thing to fix, another thing to jump into, and I have a really hard time shutting myself off.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness and impulsivity, have you ever had ongoing patterns of defying authority, breaking rules, being aggressive, or getting into serious trouble—like at school, work, or with the law—that go beyond just being impulsive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. At least not in the way you’re describing.\n\nI mean, I’ve definitely annoyed people, and I’ve been corrected plenty of times for interrupting or moving too fast or not waiting my turn, especially in structured settings. But I wouldn’t describe myself as someone who was deliberately defying authority or trying to break rules just to break them. It’s more like I would act before thinking, and then realize afterward that I’d stepped on a rule or pushed past someone’s boundary.\n\nI haven’t had, you know, a pattern of aggression or serious trouble with the law or anything like that. And at work, the problems have been more about how I come across and how hard it is for me to slow down, not me refusing to follow rules on purpose. So… no, I wouldn’t say there’s been an ongoing pattern like that beyond the impulsive stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you also struggle with staying focused on tasks—like losing track of what you're doing, getting easily distracted, or frequently forgetting details or appointments—separate from the restlessness you've described?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, I can lose the thread of what I’m doing sometimes, but when I think about what’s actually creating the problems, it feels much more tied to the restlessness and the jumping in too quickly than to, like, sitting there unable to focus.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it and say, “Yes, that’s a major pattern,” because that’s not really what I came in noticing. The thing that feels obvious to me is the constant motion and acting before I’ve really paused. With focus, I’d probably need you to ask me more specifically, because I’m not sure whether what I experience there is separate or just part of being wound up and moving too fast.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me if you often make careless mistakes in your work or lose track of details—things like misplacing paperwork, forgetting steps in a lesson plan, or losing your train of thought—even when you're not feeling restless or rushed?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 58 / special_education_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m honestly not sure I can say yes to that in a clean way. I mean, I’ve misplaced things or had moments where I lose my train of thought, sure, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed it as this separate, ongoing problem when I’m calm and not rushing.

A lot of the time, if I miss something or skip over a detail, it feels like it happens because I’m moving too fast or already thinking about the next thing. So when you ask “even when you’re not restless or rushed,” that’s harder for me to answer, because I’m not sure I have many moments where I’m really slowed down enough to compare it.

With lesson plans or paperwork, I can get frustrated because there are a lot of small pieces to keep track of, but I don’t know if I’d call it careless mistakes as a main issue. I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed a pattern of losing details apart from the being wound up and pushing ahead.

At least not in the way you’re describing.\n\nI mean, I’ve definitely annoyed people, and I’ve been corrected plenty of times for interrupting or moving too fast or not waiting my turn, especially in structured settings. But I wouldn’t describe myself as someone who was deliberately defying authority or trying to break rules just to break them. I mean, I can lose the thread of what I’m doing sometimes, but when I think about what’s actually creating the problems, it feels much more tied to the restlessness and the jumping in too quickly than to, like, sitting there unable to focus.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it and say, “Yes, that’s a major pattern,” because that’s not really what I came in noticing.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels much more like a drive to act than like worry, if that makes sense. I’m not sitting there thinking, “Oh no, what if this happens?” or running through anxious thoughts in my head most of the time. It’s more like my body and my brain are already moving before I’ve really decided to move.

What brings me in is that lately I feel like I’m just constantly running on a motor. At school, I’ll finish one thing—or sometimes I’ll barely finish it—and then I’m immediately onto the next thing. Same at home. I’ll do one task and instead of stopping or settling, I’m already looking for the next thing to handle, fix, start, check, whatever. It’s like I don’t have an off switch.

I can get stressed because of it, sure, especially when I realize I’ve been going nonstop, but I don’t think the stress is what starts it. The moving comes first. The jumping to the next thing comes first. It feels like this internal push, like I have to keep going, even when there isn’t some specific worry behind it.

        "count": 3

      }

    "traumatic_stressor": null,

      "Ruled out anxiety-driven restlessness (drive to act described as independent of worry)"

Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S014\",\"name\":\"Constant_Activity\",\"description\":\"Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\",\"reason\":\"This is the first reply of the consultation, so it should return the chief complaint symptom code.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }

Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah, um… what’s bringing me in is that lately I feel like I’m constantly running on a motor. It’s like there’s no off switch.\n\nAnd I know everybody’s busy, especially working in education, but this feels different. I’m always moving, always onto the next task, the next thought, the next thing that needs doing. Even when there isn’t something urgent, I seem to create something to do. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYes. Yeah, unfortunately it’s not just one setting. It’s showing up in both places, and that’s part of why I’m here, because I think I kept telling myself, “Well, work is just stressful,” or “home is just complicated,” but it’s bigger than that.\n\nAt work, it’s become serious. The complaints have gotten to the point where I was put on a corrective plan, which is… honestly embarrassing to say out loud. I’m a special education teacher, and I care a lot about what I do, and I’m trying to be helpful and responsive, but the way I jump in or push ahead or keep moving seems to rub people the wrong way or make them feel like I’m not listening. So now it’s not just coworkers being annoyed — it’s formal.\n\nAnd then at home, my spouse has told me pretty clearly that the caregiving routines are harder because everyone’s not just managing the actual responsibilities, they’re also managing my energy. Like I come in trying to help, but I can make the whole thing feel more chaotic. I don’t mean to, but that’s the impact.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing real problems. I feel like I’m damaging trust with people at work and at home, even when my intention is to help. That’s the part that’s been really hard to sit with.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m honestly not sure I can say yes to that in a clean way. I mean, I’ve misplaced things or had moments where I lose my train of thought, sure, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed it as this separate, ongoing problem when I’m calm and not rushing.\n\nA lot of the time, if I miss something or skip over a detail, it feels like it happens because I’m moving too fast or already thinking about the next thing.   }

Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nIt feels much more like a drive to act than like worry, if that makes sense. I’m not sitting there thinking, “Oh no, what if this happens?” or running through anxious thoughts in my head most of the time. It’s more like my body and my brain are already moving before I’ve really decided to move.\n\nWhat brings me in is that lately I feel like I’m just constantly running on a motor. At school, I’ll finish one thing—or sometimes I’ll barely finish it—and then I’m immediately onto the next thing. Same at home. I’ll do one task and instead of stopping or settling, I’m already looking for the next thing to handle, fix, start, check, whatever. It’s like I don’t have an off switch.\n\nI can get stressed because of it, sure, especially when I realize I’ve been going nonstop, but I don’t think the stress is what starts it. The moving comes first. The jumping to the next thing comes first. It feels like this internal push, like I have to keep going, even when there isn’t some specific worry behind it.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
